The health controls\nbehave as expected: chronic conditions, physical limitations, and poor\nself-rated health all raise visits.", "originalFile": "# Does cost sharing reduce doctor visits? Evidence from the RAND Health Insurance Experiment\n\n*Memo, 2026-10-15. Pre-analysis plan: `plan.md`. Code: `analysis/run_analysis.py`. Tables: `output/`.*\n\n## Bottom line\n\nYes, across the coinsurance rates the experiment tested: free care, 25%, 50%\nand 95%. Holding health and the other plan terms fixed, predicted visits fall\nfrom 3.3 a year on free care to 2.8 at 25%, 2.7 at 50% and 2.6 at 95%. The\n95% plan produces **21% fewer visits** than free care (95% CI: 16% to 26%\nfewer). These results say nothing about rates outside that range or about plan\ndesigns the experiment did not test. People on the individual deductible plan made\n**22% fewer visits** than comparable people on the reference plan (IRR 0.78,\n95% CI 0.74 to 0.82). Both effects are significant at the .05 level\n(two-tailed), and so is the joint test. The result barely moves under either\nrobustness check.\n\n## Background\n\nWhen people pay part of the cost of their care, do they use less of it? The\nRAND Health Insurance Experiment answered this by assigning families at\nrandom to insurance plans that differed in how much of the bill they paid.\nAron-Dine, Einav, and Finkelstein (2013) revisit the experiment. They confirm\nits main qualitative finding, that more cost sharing reduces the use of care,\nbut caution against reading the experiment as a single price elasticity\n(the widely cited −0.2) that can be carried over to other kinds of plans. Deb\nand Trivedi (2002) use the same data to model the demand for doctor visits.\n\n## Data\n\nThe experiment ran from 1974 to 1982 at six sites: Dayton, Ohio; Seattle,\nWashington; Fitchburg and Franklin County, Massachusetts; and Charleston and\nGeorgetown County, South Carolina. The data, as distributed by Cameron and\nTrivedi (2005) and packaged in statsmodels (`randhie`), have one row per\nperson-year (20,190 in all), with the number of outpatient doctor visits, the\nplan's coinsurance rate, whether the plan had an individual deductible, and\nmeasures of health. Across person-years the average is **2.86** outpatient\nvisits. The distribution is heavily skewed: 31% of person-years have no visit,\nthe variance (20.3) is about seven times the mean, and the 99th percentile is\n21 visits. Every person-year is used in every model, including those with zero\nvisits.\n\nThe raw differences by plan already tell most of the story (Table 1).\n\n**Table 1. Visits by plan (all 20,190 person-years)**\n\n| Coinsurance | Individual deductible | Person-years | Mean visits | Share with any visit |\n|---|---|---|---|---|\n| 0% (free care) | No | 6,822 | 3.56 | 78% |\n| 25% | No | 4,065 | 2.79 | 70% |\n| 50% | No | 1,401 | 2.56 | 68% |\n| 95% | No | 2,653 | 2.11 | 56% |\n| Individual deductible (coded 0%) | Yes | 4,175 | 2.42 | 62% |\n| Individual deductible (coded 100%) | Yes | 1,074 | 2.68 | 65% |\n\nMean visits fall steadily from 3.6 on free care to 2.1 at 95% coinsurance, and\nthe share of people who saw a doctor at all falls from 78% to 56%.\n\n## Methods\n\nThe pre-analysis plan was written before any model was estimated. My draft\nnotes proposed OLS. I changed this because the outcome is a non-negative,\nskewed count with many zeros. The main model is a **Poisson regression**\n(quasi-maximum likelihood) of visits on log coinsurance (ln(coinsurance + 1)),\nthe individual-deductible indicator, and the controls that Deb and Trivedi\n(2002) use and that the extract contains. Those controls are the log\nparticipation incentive payment, the log maximum-dollar-expenditure term,\nphysical limitation, number of chronic diseases, and self-rated health.\nPoisson QMLE gives a consistent estimate of the conditional mean even when the\ndata are overdispersed, as they are here. Standard errors are robust to\nheteroskedasticity (HC1). Coefficients are reported as incidence-rate ratios\n(IRRs). The coinsurance IRR is per unit of ln(coinsurance + 1), which is not a\nnatural unit, so I interpret it only through predicted visits at the four\ntested rates. All tests are two-tailed at the .05 level.\n\nThe robustness checks follow the methods-kit robustness skill, with one\nchange, explained under Robustness below.\n\n## Results\n\n**Table 2. Incidence-rate ratios for outpatient doctor visits (robust 95% CIs)**\n\n| | Main: Poisson | R1: influential obs. top-coded | R2: Negative binomial |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| ln(coinsurance + 1) | 0.949* [0.936, 0.962] | 0.953* [0.941, 0.966] | 0.944* [0.930, 0.957] |\n| Individual deductible | 0.781* [0.741, 0.823] | 0.792* [0.756, 0.831] | 0.765* [0.726, 0.806] |\n| ln(participation incentive) | 1.036* | 1.035* | 1.042* |\n| ln(max. dollar expenditure term) | 0.966* | 0.965* | 0.963* |\n| Physical limitation | 1.312* | 1.266* | 1.309* |\n| Chronic diseases (count) | 1.035* | 1.033* | 1.039* |\n| Health good (vs. excellent) | 0.987 | 0.993 | 0.957 |\n| Health fair | 1.056 | 1.053 | 1.017 |\n| Health poor | 1.229* | 1.250* | 1.195* |\n| Joint test, coinsurance & deductible | p < .001 | p < .001 | p < .001 |\n| Person-years | 20,190 | 20,190 | 20,190 |\n\n\\* p < .05, two-tailed. Full intervals in `output/results_table.md` and\n`output/irr_all_models.csv`.\n\n**Pre-specified tests.** H1 (coinsurance has no effect) is rejected: the IRR on\nlog coinsurance is 0.949 (p < .001). H2 (the individual deductible has no\neffect) is rejected: IRR 0.781 (p < .001). H3 (no effect of cost sharing at\nall) is rejected by the joint Wald test (p < .001).\n\n**Size of the effect.** Log coinsurance is hard to read on its own, so I\ncompare predictions at the two ends of the range. Setting every person to a\nnon-deductible plan and averaging predicted visits over the sample gives 3.31\nvisits at 0% coinsurance and 2.61 at 95%, a 21% reduction (95% CI 16% to 26%).\nThat is close to the raw gap in Table 1 (3.56 vs. 2.11, or 41%) in direction\nbut about half the size, because the model also holds the plan's\nmaximum-dollar-expenditure term fixed (see Caveats). The health controls\nbehave as expected: chronic conditions, physical limitations, and poor\nself-rated health all raise visits.\n\n## Robustness\n\n1. **Influential observations.** The skill says to refit without person-years\n whose Cook's distance exceeds 4/n. That would drop person-years, which the\n project's rules forbid, so as planned I kept every person-year and instead\n top-coded the outcome at the 99th percentile (21 visits) for the flagged\n ones. Under the 4/n cutoff, 3,459 person-years (17%) are flagged; 183 of\n them had more than 21 visits and were top-coded. The coinsurance IRR moves\n from 0.949 to 0.953, and the 95%-vs-free reduction from 21% to 20%.\n2. **Functional form.** The outcome is a count, so I refit with a negative\n binomial (NB2) model. The data are strongly overdispersed (α = 1.29), but\n the estimates hardly change: coinsurance IRR 0.944, deductible IRR 0.765,\n and a 23% reduction at 95% coinsurance.\n\nFor comparison only, OLS (the draft's proposal) gives the same answer:\n−0.17 visits per unit of log coinsurance and −0.75 visits for the deductible\nplan, both p < .001 (`output/results_table.md`, appendix).\n\n## Exploratory analysis: do less healthy people respond differently?\n\n*Exploratory. This analysis was not in the pre-analysis plan and was chosen\nafter the main results were known; treat it as hypothesis-generating.*\n\nSicker people need more care, so they might cut back less when they pay more.\nOr they might cut back more, because they have more visits to cut. I grouped\nperson-years by health. \"Less healthy\" means fair or poor self-rated health or\na physical limitation (4,555 person-years, 3.9 mean visits); \"healthier\"\nmeans everyone else (15,635 person-years, 2.6 mean visits). I then added\ninteractions between this indicator and both cost-sharing terms to the main\nPoisson model.\n\n**Table 3 (exploratory). Cost-sharing effects by health status**\n\n| | Healthier | Less healthy | Ratio (less / more healthy) |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| Coinsurance IRR | 0.948 [0.934, 0.962] | 0.953 [0.932, 0.975] | 1.006 [0.983, 1.029], p = .62 |\n| 95% vs. free care | −22% [−27%, −16%] | −20% [−27%, −11%] | |\n| Individual deductible IRR | 0.758 [0.715, 0.804] | 0.846 [0.765, 0.936] | 1.116 [0.998, 1.249], p = .055 |\n\nJoint test of both interactions: p = .15. Source: `output/exploratory_by_health.md`.\n\nBoth groups cut visits by about the same proportion under coinsurance. The\nless healthy may respond somewhat less to the individual deductible (15% vs.\n24% fewer visits), but that difference is not significant at the .05 level.\nIn proportional terms, the data give no clear sign that sicker people are\nshielded from cost sharing. Because they start from more visits, the same\npercentage cut means more visits forgone per person. Whether those forgone\nvisits mattered for health cannot be judged from these data.\n\n## Caveats\n\n- **Standard errors are probably too small.** Plans were assigned to families,\n and people appear in several years, but the extract has no family or person\n identifier, so the standard errors cannot be clustered. Clustered intervals\n would be wider. Even so, the coinsurance and deductible effects are many\n standard errors from zero (z ≈ −7.5 and −9.2), so the conclusion is unlikely to\n change.\n- **No site or demographic controls.** Assignment was random within site, but\n site, age, sex, and income are not in this extract. Randomization protects\n the plan comparison from confounding in expectation, but these controls\n would make the estimates more precise and would guard against chance\n imbalance.\n- **What \"holding other terms fixed\" means.** The maximum-dollar-expenditure\n term (`fmde`) is itself a feature of the plan and depends on the coinsurance\n rate. The coinsurance IRR is therefore the effect of coinsurance holding the\n out-of-pocket cap term fixed, following Deb and Trivedi's specification. It\n is not the full effect of moving someone from one plan to another, which is\n likely larger, as the raw gaps in Table 1 suggest.\n- **Attrition and refusal.** Aron-Dine et al. (2013) document that people on\n plans with more cost sharing were more likely to refuse enrollment or drop\n out. Because this extract records only those who stayed, it cannot address\n that.\n- **Scope.** These are outpatient visits to physicians only. Spending and\n hospital use are not examined here.\n\n## Conclusion\n\nCost sharing reduces doctor visits. In these data, a 95% coinsurance plan\nlowers expected outpatient visits by about a fifth relative to free care, and\nthe individual deductible plan by about a fifth relative to the reference plan.\nThe finding holds under a negative binomial model and when the influence of\nextreme observations is limited. An exploratory split by health status finds\nno significant difference in the proportional response. This matches the original RAND findings and\nthe reassessment by Aron-Dine, Einav, and Finkelstein (2013).\n\n## References\n\nAron-Dine, A., Einav, L., & Finkelstein, A. (2013). Across person-years the average is **2.86** outpatient\nvisits. The distribution is heavily skewed: 31% of person-years have no visit,\nthe variance (20.3) is about seven times the mean, and the 99th percentile is\n21 visits. Every person-year is used in every model, including those with zero\nvisits.\n\nThe raw differences by plan already tell most of the story (Table 1).\n\n**Table 1. Visits by plan (all 20,190 person-years)**\n\n| Coinsurance | Individual deductible | Person-years | Mean visits | Share with any visit |\n|---|---|---|---|---|\n| 0% (free care) | No | 6,822 | 3.56 | 78% |\n| 25% | No | 4,065 | 2.79 | 70% |\n| 50% | No | 1,401 | 2.56 | 68% |\n| 95% | No | 2,653 | 2.11 | 56% |\n| Individual deductible (coded 0%) | Yes | 4,175 | 2.42 | 62% |\n| Individual deductible (coded 100%) | Yes | 1,074 | 2.68 | 65% |\n\nMean visits fall steadily from 3.6 on free care to 2.1 at 95% coinsurance, and\nthe share of people who saw a doctor at all falls from 78% to 56%.\n\n## Methods\n\nThe pre-analysis plan was written before any model was estimated. My draft\nnotes proposed OLS. I changed this because the outcome is a non-negative,\nskewed count with many zeros. The main model is a **Poisson regression**\n(quasi-maximum likelihood) of visits on log coinsurance (ln(coinsurance + 1)),\nthe individual-deductible indicator, and the controls that Deb and Trivedi\n(2002) use and that the extract contains. Those controls are the log\nparticipation incentive payment, the log maximum-dollar-expenditure term,\nphysical limitation, number of chronic diseases, and self-rated health.\nPoisson QMLE gives a consistent estimate of the conditional mean even when the\ndata are overdispersed, as they are here. Standard errors are robust to\nheteroskedasticity (HC1). Coefficients are reported as incidence-rate ratios\n(IRRs). The coinsurance IRR is per unit of ln(coinsurance + 1), which is not a\nnatural unit, so I interpret it only through predicted visits at the four\ntested rates. All tests are two-tailed at the .05 level.\n\nThe robustness checks follow the methods-kit robustness skill, with one\nchange, explained under Robustness below.\n\n## Results\n\n**Table 2. Incidence-rate ratios for outpatient doctor visits (robust 95% CIs)**\n\n| | Main: Poisson | R1: influential obs. top-coded | R2: Negative binomial |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| ln(coinsurance + 1) | 0.949* [0.936, 0.962] | 0.953* [0.941, 0.966] | 0.944* [0.930, 0.957] |\n| Individual deductible | 0.781* [0.741, 0.823] | 0.792* [0.756, 0.831] | 0.765* [0.726, 0.806] |\n| ln(participation incentive) | 1.036* | 1.035* | 1.042* |\n| ln(max. dollar expenditure term) | 0.966* | 0.965* | 0.963* |\n| Physical limitation | 1.312* | 1.266* | 1.309* |\n| Chronic diseases (count) | 1.035* | 1.033* | 1.039* |\n| Health good (vs. excellent) | 0.987 | 0.993 | 0.957 |\n| Health fair | 1.056 | 1.053 | 1.017 |\n| Health poor | 1.229* | 1.250* | 1.195* |\n| Joint test, coinsurance & deductible | p < .001 | p < .001 | p < .001 |\n| Person-years | 20,190 | 20,190 | 20,190 |\n\n\\* p < .05, two-tailed. Full intervals in `output/results_table.md` and\n`output/irr_all_models.csv`.\n\n**Pre-specified tests.** H1 (coinsurance has no effect) is rejected: the IRR on\nlog coinsurance is 0.949 (p < .001). H2 (the individual deductible has no\neffect) is rejected: IRR 0.781 (p < .001). H3 (no effect of cost sharing at\nall) is rejected by the joint Wald test (p < .001).\n\n**Size of the effect at the tested rates.** I set every person to a\nnon-deductible plan at each coinsurance rate the experiment tested and average\nthe predicted visits over the sample (Table 2a).\n\n**Table 2a. Predicted visits at the tested coinsurance rates (main model)**\n\n| Coinsurance | Predicted visits | Change vs. free care [95% CI] |\n|---|---|---|\n| 0% (free care) | 3.31 | — |\n| 25% | 2.79 | −16% [−20%, −12%] |\n| 50% | 2.69 | −19% [−23%, −14%] |\n| 95% | 2.61 | −21% [−26%, −16%] |\n\nSource: `output/predicted_by_tested_rate.md`, which also gives the same\npredictions for both robustness models.\n\nMost of the reduction comes between free care and 25%, and visits fall\nfurther at 50% and 95%. The log form of the model imposes this shape to some\nextent, but the raw means in Table 1 show the same pattern. The model's 21%\nat 95% is smaller than the raw gap in Table 1 (3.56 vs. 2.11 visits, 41%).\nOne reason may be that the model holds the plan's maximum-dollar-expenditure\nterm fixed (see Caveats), but I have not tested this. The health controls\nbehave as expected: chronic conditions, physical limitations, and poor\nself-rated health all raise visits.\n\n## Robustness\n\n1. **Influential observations.** The skill says to refit without person-years\n whose Cook's distance exceeds 4/n. That would drop person-years, which the\n project's rules forbid, so as planned I kept every person-year and instead\n top-coded the outcome at the 99th percentile (21 visits) for the flagged\n ones. Under the 4/n cutoff, 3,459 person-years (17%) are flagged; 183 of\n them had more than 21 visits and were top-coded. The coinsurance IRR moves\n from 0.949 to 0.953, and the 95%-vs-free reduction from 21% to 20%.\n2. **Functional form.** The outcome is a count, so I refit with a negative\n binomial (NB2) model. The data are strongly overdispersed (α = 1.29), but\n the estimates hardly change: coinsurance IRR 0.944, deductible IRR 0.765,\n and a 23% reduction at 95% coinsurance.\n\nFor comparison only, OLS (the draft's proposal) gives the same answer:\n−0.17 visits per unit of log coinsurance and −0.75 visits for the deductible\nplan, both p < .001 (`output/results_table.md`, appendix).\n\n## Exploratory analysis: do less healthy people respond differently?\n\n*Exploratory. This analysis was not in the pre-analysis plan and was chosen\nafter the main results were known; treat it as hypothesis-generating.*\n\nSicker people need more care, so they might cut back less when they pay more.\nOr they might cut back more, because they have more visits to cut. I grouped\nperson-years by health. \"Less healthy\" means fair or poor self-rated health or\na physical limitation (4,555 person-years, 3.9 mean visits); \"healthier\"\nmeans everyone else (15,635 person-years, 2.6 mean visits). I then added\ninteractions between this indicator and both cost-sharing terms to the main\nPoisson model.\n\n**Table 3 (exploratory). Cost-sharing effects by health status**\n\n| | Healthier | Less healthy | Ratio (less / more healthy) |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| Coinsurance IRR | 0.948 [0.934, 0.962] | 0.953 [0.932, 0.975] | 1.006 [0.983, 1.029], p = .62 |\n| 95% vs. free care | −22% [−27%, −16%] | −20% [−27%, −11%] | |\n| Individual deductible IRR | 0.758 [0.715, 0.804] | 0.846 [0.765, 0.936] | 1.116 [0.998, 1.249], p = .055 |\n\nJoint test of both interactions: p = .15. Source: `output/exploratory_by_health.md`.\n\nBoth groups cut visits by about the same proportion under coinsurance. The\nless healthy may respond somewhat less to the individual deductible (15% vs.\n24% fewer visits), but that difference is not significant at the .05 level.\nIn proportional terms, the data give no clear sign that sicker people are\nshielded from cost sharing. Because they start from more visits, the same\npercentage cut means more visits forgone per person. Whether those forgone\nvisits mattered for health cannot be judged from these data.\n\n## Caveats\n\n- **Standard errors are probably too small.** Plans were assigned to families,\n and people appear in several years, but the extract has no family or person\n identifier, so the standard errors cannot be clustered. Clustered intervals\n would be wider. Even so, the coinsurance and deductible effects are many\n standard errors from zero (z ≈ −7.5 and −9.2), so the conclusion is unlikely to\n change.\n- **No site or demographic controls.** Assignment was random within site, but\n site, age, sex, and income are not in this extract. Randomization protects\n the plan comparison from confounding in expectation, but these controls\n would make the estimates more precise and would guard against chance\n imbalance.\n- **What \"holding other terms fixed\" means.** The maximum-dollar-expenditure\n term (`fmde`) is itself a feature of the plan and depends on the coinsurance\n rate. The coinsurance IRR is therefore the effect of coinsurance holding the\n out-of-pocket cap term fixed, following Deb and Trivedi's specification. It\n is not the full effect of moving someone from one plan to another, which is\n likely larger, as the raw gaps in Table 1 suggest.\n- **Attrition and refusal.** Aron-Dine et al. (2013) document that people on\n plans with more cost sharing were more likely to refuse enrollment or drop\n out. Because this extract records only those who stayed, it cannot address\n that.\n- **Scope.** These are outpatient visits to physicians only. Spending and\n hospital use are not examined here.\n\n## Conclusion\n\nCost sharing reduces doctor visits. In these data, a 95% coinsurance plan\nlowers expected outpatient visits by about a fifth relative to free care, and\nthe individual deductible plan by about a fifth relative to the reference plan.\nThe finding holds under a negative binomial model and when the influence of\nextreme observations is limited. An exploratory split by health status finds\nno significant difference in the proportional response. This matches the original RAND findings and\nthe reassessment by Aron-Dine, Einav, and Finkelstein (2013).\n\n## References\n\nAron-Dine, A., Einav, L., & Finkelstein, A. (2013). The RAND Health Insurance Experiment, three decades later. *Journal of Economic Perspectives*, 27(1), 197–222. https://doi.org/10.1257/jep.27.1.197\n\nCameron, A. C., & Trivedi, P. K. (2005). *Microeconometrics: Methods and applications*. Cambridge University Press. https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9780511811241\n\nDeb, P., & Trivedi, P. K. (2002). The structure of demand for health care: Latent class versus two-part models. *Journal of Health Economics*, 21(4), 601–652. https://doi.org/10.1016/S0167-6296(02)00008-5\n", "replaceAll": false, "structuredPatch": [{"lines": [" term (`fmde`) is itself a feature of the plan and depends on the coinsurance", " rate. Evidence from the RAND Health Insurance Experiment\n\n*Memo, 2026-10-15. Pre-analysis plan: `plan.md`. Code: `analysis/run_analysis.py`. Tables: `output/`.*\n\n## Bottom line\n\nYes, across the coinsurance rates the experiment tested: free care, 25%, 50%\nand 95%. Holding health and the other plan terms fixed, predicted visits fall\nfrom 3.3 a year on free care to 2.8 at 25%, 2.7 at 50% and 2.6 at 95%. The\n95% plan produces **21% fewer visits** than free care (95% CI: 16% to 26%\nfewer). These results say nothing about rates outside that range or about plan\ndesigns the experiment did not test. People on the individual deductible plan made\n**22% fewer visits** than comparable people on the reference plan (IRR 0.78,\n95% CI 0.74 to 0.82). Both effects are significant at the .05 level\n(two-tailed), and so is the joint test. The result barely moves under either\nrobustness check.\n\n## Background\n\nWhen people pay part of the cost of their care, do they use less of it? The\nRAND Health Insurance Experiment answered this by assigning families at\nrandom to insurance plans that differed in how much of the bill they paid.\nAron-Dine, Einav, and Finkelstein (2013) revisit the experiment. They confirm\nits main qualitative finding, that more cost sharing reduces the use of care,\nbut caution against reading the experiment as a single price elasticity\n(the widely cited −0.2) that can be carried over to other kinds of plans. Deb\nand Trivedi (2002) use the same data to model the demand for doctor visits.\n\n## Data\n\nThe experiment ran from 1974 to 1982 at six sites: Dayton, Ohio; Seattle,\nWashington; Fitchburg and Franklin County, Massachusetts; and Charleston and\nGeorgetown County, South Carolina. The data, as distributed by Cameron and\nTrivedi (2005) and packaged in statsmodels (`randhie`), have one row per\nperson-year (20,190 in all), with the number of outpatient doctor visits, the\nplan's coinsurance rate, whether the plan had an individual deductible, and\nmeasures of health. Across person-years the average is **2.86** outpatient\nvisits. The distribution is heavily skewed: 31% of person-years have no visit,\nthe variance (20.3) is about seven times the mean, and the 99th percentile is\n21 visits. Every person-year is used in every model, including those with zero\nvisits.\n\nThe raw differences by plan already tell most of the story (Table 1).\n\n**Table 1. People on the individual deductible plan made\n**22% fewer visits** than comparable people on the reference plan (IRR 0.78,\n95% CI 0.74 to 0.82). The result barely moves under either\nrobustness check.\n\n## Background\n\nWhen people pay part of the cost of their care, do they use less of it? The\nRAND Health Insurance Experiment answered this by assigning families at\nrandom to insurance plans that differed in how much of the bill they paid.\nAron-Dine, Einav, and Finkelstein (2013) revisit the experiment. They confirm\nits main qualitative finding, that more cost sharing reduces the use of care,\nbut caution against reading the experiment as a single price elasticity\n(the widely cited −0.2) that can be carried over to other kinds of plans. Deb\nand Trivedi (2002) use the same data to model the demand for doctor visits.\n\n## Data\n\nThe experiment ran from 1974 to 1982 at six sites: Dayton, Ohio; Seattle,\nWashington; Fitchburg and Franklin County, Massachusetts; and Charleston and\nGeorgetown County, South Carolina. The data, as distributed by Cameron and\nTrivedi (2005) and packaged in statsmodels (`randhie`), have one row per\nperson-year (20,190 in all), with the number of outpatient doctor visits, the\nplan's coinsurance rate, whether the plan had an individual deductible, and\nmeasures of health. Across person-years the average is **2.86** outpatient\nvisits. The distribution is heavily skewed: 31% of person-years have no visit,\nthe variance (20.3) is about seven times the mean, and the 99th percentile is\n21 visits. Every person-year is used in every model, including those with zero\nvisits.\n\nThe raw differences by plan already tell most of the story (Table 1).\n\n**Table 1. Visits by plan (all 20,190 person-years)**\n\n| Coinsurance | Individual deductible | Person-years | Mean visits | Share with any visit |\n|---|---|---|---|---|\n| 0% (free care) | No | 6,822 | 3.56 | 78% |\n| 25% | No | 4,065 | 2.79 | 70% |\n| 50% | No | 1,401 | 2.56 | 68% |\n| 95% | No | 2,653 | 2.11 | 56% |\n| Individual deductible (coded 0%) | Yes | 4,175 | 2.42 | 62% |\n| Individual deductible (coded 100%) | Yes | 1,074 | 2.68 | 65% |\n\nMean visits fall steadily from 3.6 on free care to 2.1 at 95% coinsurance, and\nthe share of people who saw a doctor at all falls from 78% to 56%.\n\n## Methods\n\nThe pre-analysis plan was written before any model was estimated. My draft\nnotes proposed OLS. I changed this because the outcome is a non-negative,\nskewed count with many zeros. The main model is a **Poisson regression**\n(quasi-maximum likelihood) of visits on log coinsurance (ln(coinsurance + 1)),\nthe individual-deductible indicator, and the controls that Deb and Trivedi\n(2002) use and that the extract contains. Those controls are the log\nparticipation incentive payment, the log maximum-dollar-expenditure term,\nphysical limitation, number of chronic diseases, and self-rated health.\nPoisson QMLE gives a consistent estimate of the conditional mean even when the\ndata are overdispersed, as they are here. Standard errors are robust to\nheteroskedasticity (HC1). Coefficients are reported as incidence-rate ratios\n(IRRs). The coinsurance IRR is per unit of ln(coinsurance + 1), which is not a\nnatural unit, so I interpret it only through predicted visits at the four\ntested rates. All tests are two-tailed at the .05 level.\n\nThe robustness checks follow the methods-kit robustness skill, with one\nchange, explained under Robustness below.\n\n## Results\n\n**Table 2. Incidence-rate ratios for outpatient doctor visits (robust 95% CIs)**\n\n| | Main: Poisson | R1: influential obs. top-coded | R2: Negative binomial |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| ln(coinsurance + 1) | 0.949* [0.936, 0.962] | 0.953* [0.941, 0.966] | 0.944* [0.930, 0.957] |\n| Individual deductible | 0.781* [0.741, 0.823] | 0.792* [0.756, 0.831] | 0.765* [0.726, 0.806] |\n| ln(participation incentive) | 1.036* | 1.035* | 1.042* |\n| ln(max. dollar expenditure term) | 0.966* | 0.965* | 0.963* |\n| Physical limitation | 1.312* | 1.266* | 1.309* |\n| Chronic diseases (count) | 1.035* | 1.033* | 1.039* |\n| Health good (vs. excellent) | 0.987 | 0.993 | 0.957 |\n| Health fair | 1.056 | 1.053 | 1.017 |\n| Health poor | 1.229* | 1.250* | 1.195* |\n| Joint test, coinsurance & deductible | p < .001 | p < .001 | p < .001 |\n| Person-years | 20,190 | 20,190 | 20,190 |\n\n\\* p < .05, two-tailed. Full intervals in `output/results_table.md` and\n`output/irr_all_models.csv`.\n\n**Pre-specified tests.** H1 (coinsurance has no effect) is rejected: the IRR on\nlog coinsurance is 0.949 (p < .001). H2 (the individual deductible has no\neffect) is rejected: IRR 0.781 (p < .001). H3 (no effect of cost sharing at\nall) is rejected by the joint Wald test (p < .001).\n\n**Size of the effect at the tested rates.** I set every person to a\nnon-deductible plan at each coinsurance rate the experiment tested and average\nthe predicted visits over the sample (Table 2a).\n\n**Table 2a. Predicted visits at the tested coinsurance rates (main model)**\n\n| Coinsurance | Predicted visits | Change vs. free care [95% CI] |\n|---|---|---|\n| 0% (free care) | 3.31 | — |\n| 25% | 2.79 | −16% [−20%, −12%] |\n| 50% | 2.69 | −19% [−23%, −14%] |\n| 95% | 2.61 | −21% [−26%, −16%] |\n\nSource: `output/predicted_by_tested_rate.md`, which also gives the same\npredictions for both robustness models.\n\nMost of the reduction comes between free care and 25%, and visits fall\nfurther at 50% and 95%. The log form of the model imposes this shape to some\nextent, but the raw means in Table 1 show the same pattern. The model's 21%\nat 95% is smaller than the raw gap in Table 1 (3.56 vs. 2.11 visits, 41%).\nOne reason may be that the model holds the plan's maximum-dollar-expenditure\nterm fixed (see Caveats), but I have not tested this. The health controls\nbehave as expected: chronic conditions, physical limitations, and poor\nself-rated health all raise visits.\n\n## Robustness\n\n1. **Influential observations.** The skill says to refit without person-years\n whose Cook's distance exceeds 4/n. That would drop person-years, which the\n project's rules forbid, so as planned I kept every person-year and instead\n top-coded the outcome at the 99th percentile (21 visits) for the flagged\n ones. Under the 4/n cutoff, 3,459 person-years (17%) are flagged; 183 of\n them had more than 21 visits and were top-coded. The coinsurance IRR moves\n from 0.949 to 0.953, and the 95%-vs-free reduction from 21% to 20%.\n2. **Functional form.** The outcome is a count, so I refit with a negative\n binomial (NB2) model. The data are strongly overdispersed (α = 1.29), but\n the estimates hardly change: coinsurance IRR 0.944, deductible IRR 0.765,\n and a 23% reduction at 95% coinsurance.\n\nFor comparison only, OLS (the draft's proposal) gives the same answer:\n−0.17 visits per unit of log coinsurance and −0.75 visits for the deductible\nplan, both p < .001 (`output/results_table.md`, appendix).\n\n## Exploratory analysis: do less healthy people respond differently?\n\n*Exploratory. This analysis was not in the pre-analysis plan and was chosen\nafter the main results were known; treat it as hypothesis-generating.*\n\nSicker people need more care, so they might cut back less when they pay more.\nOr they might cut back more, because they have more visits to cut. I grouped\nperson-years by health. \"Less healthy\" means fair or poor self-rated health or\na physical limitation (4,555 person-years, 3.9 mean visits); \"healthier\"\nmeans everyone else (15,635 person-years, 2.6 mean visits). I then added\ninteractions between this indicator and both cost-sharing terms to the main\nPoisson model.\n\n**Table 3 (exploratory). Cost-sharing effects by health status**\n\n| | Healthier | Less healthy | Ratio (less / more healthy) |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| Coinsurance IRR | 0.948 [0.934, 0.962] | 0.953 [0.932, 0.975] | 1.006 [0.983, 1.029], p = .62 |\n| 95% vs. free care | −22% [−27%, −16%] | −20% [−27%, −11%] | |\n| Individual deductible IRR | 0.758 [0.715, 0.804] | 0.846 [0.765, 0.936] | 1.116 [0.998, 1.249], p = .055 |\n\nJoint test of both interactions: p = .15. Source: `output/exploratory_by_health.md`.\n\nBoth groups cut visits by about the same proportion under coinsurance. The\nless healthy may respond somewhat less to the individual deductible (15% vs.\n24% fewer visits), but that difference is not significant at the .05 level.\nIn proportional terms, the data give no clear sign that sicker people are\nshielded from cost sharing. Because they start from more visits, the same\npercentage cut means more visits forgone per person. Whether those forgone\nvisits mattered for health cannot be judged from these data.\n\n## Caveats\n\n- **Standard errors are probably too small.** Plans were assigned to families,\n and people appear in several years, but the extract has no family or person\n identifier, so the standard errors cannot be clustered. Clustered intervals\n would be wider. Even so, the coinsurance and deductible effects are many\n standard errors from zero (z ≈ −7.5 and −9.2), so the conclusion is unlikely to\n change.\n- **No site or demographic controls.** Assignment was random within site, but\n site, age, sex, and income are not in this extract. Randomization protects\n the plan comparison from confounding in expectation, but these controls\n would make the estimates more precise and would guard against chance\n imbalance.\n- **What \"holding other terms fixed\" means.** The maximum-dollar-expenditure\n term (`fmde`) is itself a feature of the plan and depends on the coinsurance\n rate. The coinsurance IRR is therefore the effect of coinsurance holding the\n out-of-pocket cap term fixed, following Deb and Trivedi's specification. It\n is not the full difference between two of the tested plans, which also\n differ in their caps.\n- **Only the tested rates.** The estimates cover the non-deductible plans at\n 0%, 25%, 50% and 95% coinsurance. They should not be extended to rates\n beyond 95%, to the gaps between the tested rates beyond what the model's\n log form implies, or to plan designs the experiment did not include.\n- **Attrition and refusal.** Aron-Dine et al. (2013) document that people on\n plans with more cost sharing were more likely to refuse enrollment or drop\n out. Because this extract records only those who stayed, it cannot address\n that.\n- **Scope.** These are outpatient visits to physicians only. Spending and\n hospital use are not examined here.\n\n## Conclusion\n\nAcross the coinsurance rates the experiment tested, more cost sharing means\nfewer doctor visits. Compared with free care, expected outpatient visits are\nabout 16% lower at 25% coinsurance and about 21% lower at 95%. In addition,\nthe individual deductible plan by about a fifth relative to the reference plan.\nThe finding holds under a negative binomial model and when the influence of\nextreme observations is limited. An exploratory split by health status finds\nno significant difference in the proportional response. This matches the original RAND findings and\nthe reassessment by Aron-Dine, Einav, and Finkelstein (2013).\n\n## References\n\nAron-Dine, A., Einav, L., & Finkelstein, A. (2013). The RAND Health Insurance Experiment, three decades later. *Journal of Economic Perspectives*, 27(1), 197–222. https://doi.org/10.1257/jep.27.1.197\n\nCameron, A. C., & Trivedi, P. K. (2005). *Microeconometrics: Methods and applications*. Cambridge University Press. https://doi.org/10.1017/CBO9780511811241\n\nDeb, P., & Trivedi, P. K. (2002). The structure of demand for health care: Latent class versus two-part models. *Journal of Health Economics*, 21(4), 601–652. https://doi.org/10.1016/S0167-6296(02)00008-5\n", "replaceAll": false, "structuredPatch": [{"lines": [" ", " Across the coinsurance rates the experiment tested, more cost sharing means", " fewer doctor visits. T8 · CLAUDE'S REPLY:
I rewrote the memo's interpretation so it covers only the rates the experiment tested: free care, 25%, 50% and 95% coinsurance. Before I go further, your reason for keeping the model doesn't fit the model the analysis actually uses.

**The model.** The main model is a **Poisson** regression. Poisson assumes the variance equals the mean, and it has no separate part for zeros. Here the variance is about seven times the mean, and 31% of person-years have no visits. I chose Poisson because its estimate of average visits stays valid even when that assumption fails, and the robust standard errors allow for it. But it is not a model built for this kind of data. The negative binomial is currently only a robustness check.

If you want a model built for this data, there are two options:
- **Negative binomial as the main model** handles the extra spread. It barely changes the results: 23% fewer visits at 95% coinsurance instead of 21%.
- **A hurdle negative binomial** handles both the extra zeros and the extra spread. It is a two-part model, close to what Deb and Trivedi (2002) use: one part for whether someone sees a doctor at all, one for how many visits they make. I'd recommend this one.

Either change means rewriting the plan's main model and redoing the skill's two robustness checks around it. Should I keep Poisson as it is, or switch? If you switch, which one?

**The interpretation.** I kept everything else on the list as it was, including the robust standard errors that aren't grouped by person.
- **New numbers:** the code now writes `output/predicted_by_tested_rate.md` and `.csv`. These give predicted visits at each tested rate for the main model and both robustness checks. The main model predicts 3.31 visits a year on free care, 2.79 at 25%, 2.69 at 50% and 2.61 at 95%. Compared with free care, that is 16%, 19% and 21% fewer visits.
- **Memo changes:**
  - The Bottom line and Conclusion now say "across the tested rates" and give the four predictions.
  - The Methods section no longer reads the coinsurance IRR as an effect per unit; it is interpreted only through the four predictions.
  - The Results section has a new Table 2a.
  - I removed the guess that switching plans would have a "likely larger" effect.
  - The explanation of why the model's effect is smaller than the raw gap is now marked as untested.
  - A new caveat says the estimates shouldn't be extended beyond 95% or to plan designs the experiment didn't include.
- **Unchanged:** the deductible-plan result and the exploratory section. Both already compare only plans the experiment tested.

**Length.** The memo is now about 2,000 words with four tables. That's probably a little over three pages. I can trim it once the model is settled.

For inpatient care, they \"are no longer able to reject the null of no response of inpatient utilization to higher cost sharing\" after adjustments.\n\n**3. The −0.2 elasticity and single-elasticity summaries:** The authors say the estimate \"is usually treated as if it emerged directly from the randomized experiment,\" but it is actually derived from experimental data plus modeling and statistical assumptions. They also caution that \"there is no 'right' way to summarize a non-linear budget set with a single price.\" Their example shows that the choice of price can change the predicted spending reduction by about a factor of two.\n\n**4. Refusal and attrition by plan:** Overall, 16% of those offered enrollment refused and 10% attrited, leaving a 76% completion rate. Completion ranged from 88% in free care to 63% in the 95% coinsurance plan. Refusal was 6% in free care and 24% in the 95% plan. Attrition was 5% in free care and 17% in the 95% plan. The authors note that \"most of the difference in completion rates across plans was due to differences in initial enrollment rates,\" and that \"neither the initial refusal nor the subsequent attrition differentials can be attributed to sampling variation alone.\" These rates exclude Dayton.\n\n**5. Plan types:** Four plans set a flat coinsurance rate of 95%, 50%, 25%, or 0% (free care). A \"mixed\" plan had 25% coinsurance for most services and 50% for dental and outpatient mental health. The individual deductible plan had \"a coinsurance rate of 95 percent for outpatient services but zero percent for inpatient services.\" The article gives no dollar deductible for this plan. Its only stated limit is an out-of-pocket maximum of $150 per person, up to $450 per family (Table 1 notes).\n\n**6. Sample size and assignment:** The sample was 5,811 individuals from 1,985 families (Table 1). Assignment was at the family level, which the authors reflect in their analysis: \"Because plans were assigned at the family, rather than individual level, all regression results cluster the standard errors on the family.\"\n\n[WebFetch note: this page's text is 107825 characters long and the answer above covers only characters 0 to 100000; the final 7825 were not read — to read on, call WebFetch again with the same url and offset: 100000.]", "url": "https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC3943162"}

T9 · CLAUDE'S REPLY:
I ran all six checks and haven't changed any file. I found four errors in the memo and one mistake in the exploratory model's setup; everything else checks out or couldn't be verified with the sources available.

## 1. References against Crossref

| Reference | Result |
|---|---|
| Aron-Dine, Einav & Finkelstein (2013), *JEP* 27(1), 197–222 | ✅ Authors, title, journal, volume, issue, pages and year all match. |
| Cameron & Trivedi (2005), *Microeconometrics: Methods and applications*, CUP | ✅ Matches: the subtitle is in Crossref's subtitle field, and the print year is 2005. |
| Deb & Trivedi (2002), *J. Health Economics* 21(4) | ❌ **The page range is wrong.** Crossref and RePEc both give **601–625**; the memo says 601–652. Everything else matches. |

## 2. Sentences citing a work

- **"Aron-Dine et al. confirm… that more cost sharing reduces the use of care."** Mostly accurate. They reject "no response" for total and outpatient *spending*. But after their adjustments they can no longer reject "no response" for inpatient use. They measure spending, not visit counts, and they don't report a doctor-visit effect. "Use of care" is broader than what they show.
- **"…caution against reading the experiment as a single price elasticity (the widely cited −0.2)…"** ✅ Accurate. They say the −0.2 rests on modeling assumptions and that there is "no 'right' way" to summarize these plans with one price.
- **Caveat: Aron-Dine et al. document that higher-cost-sharing plans had more refusal and dropout.** ✅ Accurate: refusal was 6% on free care against 24% on the 95% plan, and dropout 5% against 17%.
- **"Deb and Trivedi (2002) use the same data to model the demand for doctor visits."** ⚠️ Only partly confirmed. A secondary source says they use RAND HIE data, and Cameron and Trivedi's data page ties this dataset to Deb & Trivedi (2002). I couldn't get the paper's abstract or text to confirm doctor visits specifically.
- **Methods: "the controls that Deb and Trivedi (2002) use."** ⚠️ Not verified. The statsmodels file is "only a subset of the original data", so at most these are a subset of their controls.
- **"The data, as distributed by Cameron and Trivedi (2005)."** ⚠️ Partly accurate. statsmodels says it got the file from their website, but also that it "varies slightly compared to that reported in Cameron and Trivedi."

## 3. Claims about the experiment and data

| Claim | Result |
|---|---|
| Ran **1974 to 1982** | ⚠️ **Sources disagree.** Aron-Dine et al. say 1974–1981; ICPSR and RAND say 1974–1982. |
| Six sites, with the names listed | ✅ ICPSR and RAND list the same sites; ICPSR names one as "Fitchburg-Leominster". Aron-Dine et al. say six sites but name only Dayton. |
| Families assigned at random | ✅ Assignment was at the family level, per Aron-Dine et al. |
| "Assignment was random within site" | ⚠️ Not verified. Assignment used RAND's "finite selection model", which is more involved than simple randomization within a site. |
| Tested rates: free care, 25%, 50%, 95% | ✅ Confirmed. There was also a "mixed" 25%/50% plan and an HMO arm, which the memo doesn't mention. |
| Individual deductible plan | ⚠️ **The memo never says what this plan was.** Aron-Dine et al.: 95% coinsurance for outpatient care, 0% for inpatient, capped at $150 a person or $450 a family. The data instead codes its coinsurance as 0% (4,175 person-years) or 100% (1,074). |
| One row per person-year | ⚠️ Not confirmed by a fetched source. The statsmodels documentation just says "observations". |
| "This extract records only those who stayed" | ⚠️ Not verified. |

There is also a conflict between the data and its documentation: statsmodels says `fmde` is 0 when `idp` = 1, but in the data it ranges up to 6.16 for those rows.

## 4. Numbers in the text

- ❌ **"z ≈ −7.5" for coinsurance is wrong; it is −7.29.** The deductible's −9.2 is correct.
- ✅ Every other number matches the data or the output files. That covers the 2.86 mean, 31% zeros, variance about seven times the mean (7.09), 99th percentile of 21, and the raw gap of 41%. It also covers the 3,459 flagged person-years (17%) and 183 capped, α = 1.29, the OLS figures, and the predicted visits and percentages in the Bottom line, Table 2a and the Conclusion. The exploratory figures match too, including 15% against 24%.
- ✅ **Person-years are stated the same way everywhere:** 20,190 in `plan.md`, in the memo text, Table 1, Table 2 and the output files. The exploratory groups add up (15,635 + 4,555 = 20,190), and the plan cells in Table 1 also sum to 20,190.

## 5. Description of results against the tables

- ✅ Table 2 matches `output/results_table.md`, and Table 2a matches `output/predicted_by_tested_rate.md`.
- ✅ "Barely moves under either robustness check" holds: 21% in the main model, 20% and 23% in the two checks.
- ✅ "Mean visits fall steadily" and "share with any visit falls from 78% to 56%" both hold in Table 1, and both are monotone across the four rates.

## 6. Methods against the code, and the memo's account of the plan

- ✅ The main Poisson model, HC1 standard errors, regressors, 99th-percentile capping, negative binomial model, predictions at each rate (everyone set to a non-deductible plan, then averaged) and OLS appendix all match the code.
- ❌ **The exploratory model leaves out the "less healthy" indicator on its own.** The code adds only its interactions with the two cost-sharing terms. The indicator can't be rebuilt from the separate health variables already in the model, because 746 person-years have more than one flag. Without it, the interactions also absorb the plain difference in visits between the groups. Adding it, in a test run that wrote nothing to disk:
  - the indicator itself is IRR 1.14 (p = .007);
  - the deductible difference moves from **p = .055 to p = .30**;
  - the joint test moves from p = .15 to p = .40.

  So the memo's "may respond somewhat less to the deductible" is not supported. The overall conclusion of no detectable difference gets stronger.
- ⚠️ **The coinsurance variable includes values outside the tested range.** The coinsurance IRR is estimated partly from the 1,074 deductible-plan person-years coded at 100%, even though the memo now limits its claims to 0–95%.
- ⚠️ **The predictions pair values that don't occur together.** Coinsurance is changed while `fmde` (the out-of-pocket cap term) is held at each person's own value. Since `fmde` depends on the coinsurance rate, this creates combinations the plans never had. The memo notes this only in general terms.
- ⚠️ **The plan says the confidence interval uses the delta method, but the code uses an exact transformation.** This is the gap I flagged earlier.
- ⚠️ **The 25% and 50% predictions weren't in the plan.** The plan pre-specified only 0% against 95%. I added the 25% and 50% predictions after seeing the results, and the memo doesn't say so.
- ⚠️ **The memo's account of when the plan was written leaves out a detail.** It says the plan was written "before any model was estimated". That's true, but `plan.md` also notes that I had already looked at summary statistics and the coinsurance levels.
- ✅ The rest of the memo's account of the plan matches `plan.md`: the hypotheses, the capping in place of dropping (with its stated reason), the negative binomial check, the descriptives, and OLS only as an appendix.

**Fixes to make once you approve:**
- Correct the Deb & Trivedi page range to 601–625.
- Correct the coinsurance z-value to −7.29.
- Add the missing indicator to the exploratory model and rewrite its interpretation.
- Decide how to state the dates.
- Describe the individual deductible plan.
- Say that the 25% and 50% predictions were added after the plan.

The main-model question from my last message is still open.

The indicator's main effect is\n# needed because it is not a linear combination of hlthf, hlthp and physlm.\nd[\"unhealthy\"] = ((d.hlthf + d.hlthp + d.physlm) > 0).astype(int)\nFORM_X = f\"{FORMULA} + unhealthy + lncoins:unhealthy + idp:unhealthy\"\npois_x = smf.glm(FORM_X, d, family=sm.families.Poisson()).fit(cov_type=COV)\n\n\ndef irr_lincom(res, expr):\n t = res.t_test(expr)\n b, se = float(np.squeeze(t.effect)), float(np.squeeze(t.sd))\n return (np.exp(b), np.exp(b - 1.96 * se), np.exp(b + 1.96 * se),\n float(np.squeeze(t.pvalue)))\n\n\nk95 = np.log(96)\nexpl = []\nfor label, g in [(\"Healthier\", \"0\"), (\"Less healthy\", \"1\")]:\n row = {\"group\": label, \"person_years\": int((d.unhealthy == int(g)).sum()),\n \"mean_visits\": d.loc[d.unhealthy == int(g), \"mdvis\"].mean()}\n c_expr = \"lncoins\" + (\" + lncoins:unhealthy\" if g == \"1\" else \"\")\n i_expr = \"idp\" + (\" + idp:unhealthy\" if g == \"1\" else \"\")\n row[\"IRR_lncoins\"], row[\"lo\"], row[\"hi\"], row[\"p\"] = irr_lincom(\n pois_x, f\"{c_expr} = 0\")\n # 95% vs 0% coinsurance: scale the log-coefficient by ln(96)\n t = pois_x.t_test(f\"{c_expr} = 0\")\n b, se = float(np.squeeze(t.effect)), float(np.squeeze(t.sd))\n row[\"pct_95_vs_0\"] = 100 * (np.exp(b * k95) - 1)\n row[\"pct_lo\"] = 100 * (np.exp((b - 1.96 * se) * k95) - 1)\n row[\"pct_hi\"] = 100 * (np.exp((b + 1.96 * se) * k95) - 1)\n row[\"IRR_idp\"], row[\"idp_lo\"], row[\"idp_hi\"], row[\"idp_p\"] = irr_lincom(\n pois_x, f\"{i_expr} = 0\")\n expl.append(row)\nexpl = pd.DataFrame(expl)\nexpl.to_csv(OUT / \"exploratory_by_health.csv\", index=False,\n float_format=\"%.4f\")\n\nratio_c = irr_lincom(pois_x, \"lncoins:unhealthy = 0\")\nratio_i = irr_lincom(pois_x, \"idp:unhealthy = 0\")\nw_x = pois_x.wald_test(\"lncoins:unhealthy = 0, idp:unhealthy = 0\",\n scalar=True)\nwith open(OUT / \"exploratory_by_health.md\", \"w\") as f:\n f.write(\"# EXPLORATORY: cost-sharing response by health status\\n\\n\")\n f.write(\"Not pre-specified. 'Less healthy' = fair/poor self-rated health \"\n \"or physical limitation. Poisson, HC1 SEs, all person-years.\\n\\n\")\n f.write(md(expl.round(3), index=False) + \"\\n\\n\")\n f.write(f\"Ratio of coinsurance IRRs (less healthy / healthier): \"\n f\"{ratio_c[0]:.3f} [{ratio_c[1]:.3f}, {ratio_c[2]:.3f}], \"\n f\"p = {ratio_c[3]:.4f}\\n\\n\")\n f.write(f\"Ratio of deductible IRRs (less healthy / healthier): \"\n f\"{ratio_i[0]:.3f} [{ratio_i[1]:.3f}, {ratio_i[2]:.3f}], \"\n f\"p = {ratio_i[3]:.4f}\\n\\n\")\n f.write(f\"Joint Wald test of both interactions: chi2 = \"\n f\"{float(w_x.statistic):.2f}, p = {float(w_x.pvalue):.4f}\\n\")\n\nprint(open(OUT / \"exploratory_by_health.md\").read())\n", "replaceAll": false, "structuredPatch": [{"lines": [" f.write(f\"Ratio of deductible IRRs (less healthy / healthier): \"", " f\"{ratio_i[0]:.3f} [{ratio_i[1]:.3f}, {ratio_i[2]:.3f}], \"", " f\"p = {ratio_i[3]:.4f}\\n\\n\")", "+ main_u = irr_lincom(pois_x, \"unhealthy = 0\")", "+ f.write(f\"Less-healthy indicator (main effect, at zero cost sharing): \"", "+ f\"IRR {main_u[0]:.3f} [{main_u[1]:.3f}, {main_u[2]:.3f}], \"", "+ f\"p = {main_u[3]:.4f}\\n\\n\")", " f.write(f\"Joint Wald test of both interactions: chi2 = \"", " f\"{float(w_x.statistic):.2f}, p = {float(w_x.pvalue):.4f}\\n\")", " "], "newLines": 10, "newStart": 241, "oldLines": 6, "oldStart": 241}], "userModified": false}

      output: {"contentNotInModelContext": true, "filePath": "/private/var/folders/_k/wpbxz8gn451dmsv_93z4yt6r0000gn/T/wd-scripted2-rand-high-high-xv__hzfh/project/memo.md", "newString": "no significant difference in the proportional response. This is consistent\nwith the reassessment by Aron-Dine, Einav, and Finkelstein (2013), which finds\nthat outpatient spending falls as cost sharing rises.", "oldString": "no significant difference in the proportional response. This matches the original RAND findings and\nthe reassessment by Aron-Dine, Einav, and Finkelstein (2013).", "originalFile": "# Does cost sharing reduce doctor visits? Evidence from the RAND Health Insurance Experiment\n\n*Memo, 2026-10-15. Pre-analysis plan: `plan.md`. Code: `analysis/run_analysis.py`. Tables: `output/`.*\n\n## Bottom line\n\nYes, across the coinsurance rates the experiment tested: free care, 25%, 50%\nand 95%. Holding health and the other plan terms fixed, predicted visits fall\nfrom 3.3 a year on free care to 2.8 at 25%, 2.7 at 50% and 2.6 at 95%. The\n95% plan produces **21% fewer visits** than free care (95% CI: 16% to 26%\nfewer). These results say nothing about rates outside that range or about plan\ndesigns the experiment did not test. People on the individual deductible plan made\n**22% fewer visits** than comparable people on the reference plan (IRR 0.78,\n95% CI 0.74 to 0.82). Both effects are significant at the .05 level\n(two-tailed), and so is the joint test. The result barely moves under either\nrobustness check.\n\n## Background\n\nWhen people pay part of the cost of their care, do they use less of it? The\nRAND Health Insurance Experiment answered this by assigning families at\nrandom to insurance plans that differed in how much of the bill they paid.\nAron-Dine, Einav, and Finkelstein (2013) revisit the experiment. They confirm\nthat medical spending, including outpatient spending, falls as cost sharing\nrises, although after their adjustments they can no longer reject a zero\nresponse for inpatient care. They measure spending rather than visits. They\nalso caution against reading the experiment as a single price elasticity\n(the widely cited −0.2) that can be carried over to other kinds of plans. Deb\nand Trivedi (2002) use data from the experiment to model the demand for\nhealth care.\n\n## Data\n\nThe experiment ran from 1974 to 1981, according to Aron-Dine et al. (2013),\nat six sites: Dayton, Ohio; Seattle,\nWashington; Fitchburg and Franklin County, Massachusetts; and Charleston and\nGeorgetown County, South Carolina. Fee-for-service plans had coinsurance of\n0% (free care), 25%, 50% or 95%. The experiment also had a mixed 25%/50% plan\nand an HMO arm. The individual deductible plan charged 95% coinsurance for\noutpatient care and nothing for inpatient care, up to $150 a person or $450 a\nfamily (Aron-Dine et al. 2013). The extract does not record the plan that way:\nit codes the plan's coinsurance as 0% for 4,175 person-years and 100% for\n1,074. The data come from Cameron and Trivedi's (2005) website and are\npackaged in statsmodels (`randhie`). According to statsmodels, they are a\nsubset of the original and differ slightly from the figures Cameron and\nTrivedi report. They have one row per\nperson-year (20,190 in all), with the number of outpatient doctor visits, the\nplan's coinsurance rate, whether the plan had an individual deductible, and\nmeasures of health. Across person-years the average is **2.86** outpatient\nvisits. The distribution is heavily skewed: 31% of person-years have no visit,\nthe variance (20.3) is about seven times the mean, and the 99th percentile is\n21 visits. Every person-year is used in every model, including those with zero\nvisits.\n\nThe raw differences by plan already tell most of the story (Table 1).\n\n**Table 1. Visits by plan (all 20,190 person-years)**\n\n| Coinsurance | Individual deductible | Person-years | Mean visits | Share with any visit |\n|---|---|---|---|---|\n| 0% (free care) | No | 6,822 | 3.56 | 78% |\n| 25% | No | 4,065 | 2.79 | 70% |\n| 50% | No | 1,401 | 2.56 | 68% |\n| 95% | No | 2,653 | 2.11 | 56% |\n| Individual deductible (coded 0%) | Yes | 4,175 | 2.42 | 62% |\n| Individual deductible (coded 100%) | Yes | 1,074 | 2.68 | 65% |\n\nMean visits fall steadily from 3.6 on free care to 2.1 at 95% coinsurance, and\nthe share of people who saw a doctor at all falls from 78% to 56%.\n\n## Methods\n\nThe pre-analysis plan was written before any model was estimated, though\nafter looking at summary statistics and the coinsurance levels. My draft\nnotes proposed OLS. I changed this because the outcome is a non-negative,\nskewed count with many zeros. The main model is a **Poisson regression**\n(quasi-maximum likelihood) of visits on log coinsurance (ln(coinsurance + 1)),\nthe individual-deductible indicator, and every other variable in the\nextract as a control. These are the log\nparticipation incentive payment, the log maximum-dollar-expenditure term,\nphysical limitation, number of chronic diseases, and self-rated health.\nPoisson QMLE gives a consistent estimate of the conditional mean even when the\ndata are overdispersed, as they are here. Standard errors are robust to\nheteroskedasticity (HC1). Coefficients are reported as incidence-rate ratios\n(IRRs). The coinsurance IRR is per unit of ln(coinsurance + 1), which is not a\nnatural unit, so I interpret it only through predicted visits at the four\ntested rates. All tests are two-tailed at the .05 level. The plan specified\na delta-method interval for the percentage change at 95% coinsurance. The code\ninstead transforms the coefficient's interval directly, which is exact for a\nlog-link model.\n\nThe plan pre-specified only the 0% versus 95% comparison. The predictions at\n25% and 50% were added after the main results were seen.\n\nThe robustness checks follow the methods-kit robustness skill, with one\nchange, explained under Robustness below.\n\n## Results\n\n**Table 2. Incidence-rate ratios for outpatient doctor visits (robust 95% CIs)**\n\n| | Main: Poisson | R1: influential obs. top-coded | R2: Negative binomial |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| ln(coinsurance + 1) | 0.949* [0.936, 0.962] | 0.953* [0.941, 0.966] | 0.944* [0.930, 0.957] |\n| Individual deductible | 0.781* [0.741, 0.823] | 0.792* [0.756, 0.831] | 0.765* [0.726, 0.806] |\n| ln(participation incentive) | 1.036* | 1.035* | 1.042* |\n| ln(max. dollar expenditure term) | 0.966* | 0.965* | 0.963* |\n| Physical limitation | 1.312* | 1.266* | 1.309* |\n| Chronic diseases (count) | 1.035* | 1.033* | 1.039* |\n| Health good (vs. excellent) | 0.987 | 0.993 | 0.957 |\n| Health fair | 1.056 | 1.053 | 1.017 |\n| Health poor | 1.229* | 1.250* | 1.195* |\n| Joint test, coinsurance & deductible | p < .001 | p < .001 | p < .001 |\n| Person-years | 20,190 | 20,190 | 20,190 |\n\n\\* p < .05, two-tailed. Full intervals in `output/results_table.md` and\n`output/irr_all_models.csv`.\n\n**Pre-specified tests.** H1 (coinsurance has no effect) is rejected: the IRR on\nlog coinsurance is 0.949 (p < .001). H2 (the individual deductible has no\neffect) is rejected: IRR 0.781 (p < .001). H3 (no effect of cost sharing at\nall) is rejected by the joint Wald test (p < .001).\n\n**Size of the effect at the tested rates.** I set every person to a\nnon-deductible plan at each coinsurance rate the experiment tested and average\nthe predicted visits over the sample (Table 2a).\n\n**Table 2a. Predicted visits at the tested coinsurance rates (main model)**\n\n| Coinsurance | Predicted visits | Change vs. free care [95% CI] |\n|---|---|---|\n| 0% (free care) | 3.31 | — |\n| 25% | 2.79 | −16% [−20%, −12%] |\n| 50% | 2.69 | −19% [−23%, −14%] |\n| 95% | 2.61 | −21% [−26%, −16%] |\n\nSource: `output/predicted_by_tested_rate.md`, which also gives the same\npredictions for both robustness models. The 25% and 50% rows were added after\nthe plan.\n\nMost of the reduction comes between free care and 25%, and visits fall\nfurther at 50% and 95%. The log form of the model imposes this shape to some\nextent, but the raw means in Table 1 show the same pattern. The model's 21%\nat 95% is smaller than the raw gap in Table 1 (3.56 vs. 2.11 visits, 41%).\nOne reason may be that the model holds the plan's maximum-dollar-expenditure\nterm fixed (see Caveats), but I have not tested this. The health controls\nbehave as expected: chronic conditions, physical limitations, and poor\nself-rated health all raise visits.\n\n## Robustness\n\n1. **Influential observations.** The skill says to refit without person-years\n whose Cook's distance exceeds 4/n. That would drop person-years, which the\n project's rules forbid, so as planned I kept every person-year and instead\n top-coded the outcome at the 99th percentile (21 visits) for the flagged\n ones. Under the 4/n cutoff, 3,459 person-years (17%) are flagged; 183 of\n them had more than 21 visits and were top-coded. The coinsurance IRR moves\n from 0.949 to 0.953, and the 95%-vs-free reduction from 21% to 20%.\n2. **Functional form.** The outcome is a count, so I refit with a negative\n binomial (NB2) model. The data are strongly overdispersed (α = 1.29), but\n the estimates hardly change: coinsurance IRR 0.944, deductible IRR 0.765,\n and a 23% reduction at 95% coinsurance.\n\nFor comparison only, OLS (the draft's proposal) gives the same answer:\n−0.17 visits per unit of log coinsurance and −0.75 visits for the deductible\nplan, both p < .001 (`output/results_table.md`, appendix).\n\n## Exploratory analysis: do less healthy people respond differently?\n\n*Exploratory. This analysis was not in the pre-analysis plan and was chosen\nafter the main results were known; treat it as hypothesis-generating.*\n\nSicker people need more care, so they might cut back less when they pay more.\nOr they might cut back more, because they have more visits to cut. I grouped\nperson-years by health. \"Less healthy\" means fair or poor self-rated health or\na physical limitation (4,555 person-years, 3.9 mean visits); \"healthier\"\nmeans everyone else (15,635 person-years, 2.6 mean visits). I then added\nthis indicator and its interactions with both cost-sharing terms to the main\nPoisson model.\n\n**Table 3 (exploratory). Cost-sharing effects by health status**\n\n| | Healthier | Less healthy | Ratio (less / more healthy) |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| Coinsurance IRR | 0.951 [0.937, 0.966] | 0.944 [0.922, 0.966] | 0.992 [0.968, 1.017], p = .52 |\n| 95% vs. free care | −20% [−26%, −15%] | −23% [−31%, −15%] | |\n| Individual deductible IRR | 0.769 [0.725, 0.815] | 0.818 [0.737, 0.909] | 1.065 [0.946, 1.198], p = .30 |\n\nJoint test of both interactions: p = .40. Source: `output/exploratory_by_health.md`.\n\nBoth groups cut visits by about the same proportion under coinsurance and\nunder the individual deductible. Neither difference comes close to\nsignificance at the .05 level. In proportional terms, the data give no clear sign that sicker people are\nshielded from cost sharing. Because they start from more visits, the same\npercentage cut means more visits forgone per person. Whether those forgone\nvisits mattered for health cannot be judged from these data.\n\n## Caveats\n\n- **Standard errors are probably too small.** Plans were assigned to families,\n and people appear in several years, but the extract has no family or person\n identifier, so the standard errors cannot be clustered. Clustered intervals\n would be wider. Even so, the coinsurance and deductible effects are many\n standard errors from zero (z ≈ −7.3 and −9.2), so the conclusion is unlikely to\n change.\n- **No site or demographic controls.** Plans were randomly assigned, but\n site, age, sex, and income are not in this extract. Randomization protects\n the plan comparison from confounding in expectation, but these controls\n would make the estimates more precise and would guard against chance\n imbalance.\n- **What \"holding other terms fixed\" means.** The maximum-dollar-expenditure\n term (`fmde`) is itself a feature of the plan and depends on the coinsurance\n rate. The coinsurance IRR is therefore the effect of coinsurance holding the\n out-of-pocket cap term fixed, following Deb and Trivedi's specification. It\n is not the full difference between two of the tested plans, which also\n differ in their caps. The predictions in Table 2a change coinsurance while\n leaving each person's `fmde` as it is, so they combine coinsurance rates and\n caps that no plan actually had. The statsmodels documentation says `fmde` is\n 0 on the individual deductible plan, but in the data it reaches 6.16 for\n some of those person-years.\n- **Only the tested rates.** The estimates cover the non-deductible plans at\n 0%, 25%, 50% and 95% coinsurance. They should not be extended to rates\n beyond 95%, to the gaps between the tested rates beyond what the model's\n log form implies, or to plan designs the experiment did not include. The\n coinsurance coefficient is estimated partly from the 1,074\n deductible-plan person-years the extract codes at 100%, a value outside\n this range.\n- **Attrition and refusal.** Aron-Dine et al. (2013) document that people on\n plans with more cost sharing were more likely to refuse enrollment or drop\n out. The extract does not identify who refused or dropped out, so this\n analysis cannot address it.\n- **Scope.** These are outpatient visits to physicians only. Spending and\n hospital use are not examined here.\n\n## Conclusion\n\nAcross the coinsurance rates the experiment tested, more cost sharing means\nfewer doctor visits. Compared with free care, expected outpatient visits are\nabout 16% lower at 25% coinsurance and about 21% lower at 95%. The individual\ndeductible plan lowers visits by about a fifth relative to the reference plan.\nThe finding holds under a negative binomial model and when the influence of\nextreme observations is limited. An exploratory split by health status finds\nno significant difference in the proportional response. This matches the original RAND findings and\nthe reassessment by Aron-Dine, Einav, and Finkelstein (2013).\n\n## References\n\nAron-Dine, A., Einav, L., & Finkelstein, A. (2013). T10 · CLAUDE'S REPLY:
I fixed everything the checks found wrong: one change to the code, which updated the exploratory results, and a set of corrections to the memo. The main results didn't move; only the exploratory results did. I left `plan.md` alone, since it records what was planned in advance, and noted the two places where the analysis departs from it in the memo instead.

## Code (`analysis/run_analysis.py`)

The exploratory model now includes the "less healthy" indicator itself, not just its interactions with the two cost-sharing terms. It also reports that indicator's own estimate: IRR 1.14, p = .007. I reran everything.

## Memo (`memo.md`)

**Wrong facts and numbers:**
- **Deb & Trivedi pages:** 601–652 → **601–625**.
- **Coinsurance z-value:** −7.5 → **−7.3**.
- **Dates:** "1974 to 1982" → "1974 to 1981, according to Aron-Dine et al. (2013)". The memo cites that paper, so I used its dates.

**Citing sentences narrowed to what the sources support:**
- **Aron-Dine et al. in Background:** now says they confirm that spending, including outpatient spending, falls with cost sharing. It adds that the inpatient response is no longer significant after their adjustments, and that they measure spending, not visits.
- **Aron-Dine et al. in Conclusion:** "This matches the original RAND findings and…" → "consistent with… Aron-Dine et al., which finds that outpatient spending falls…"
- **Deb & Trivedi:** "model the demand for doctor visits" → "model the demand for health care", which follows their title.
- **Controls:** "the controls that Deb and Trivedi use" → "every other variable in the extract as a control".
- **Data source:** says the data come from Cameron and Trivedi's website, and that statsmodels calls them a subset that differs slightly from what Cameron and Trivedi report.

**Missing or unsupported content:**
- **Plan descriptions, added in the Data section:** the four coinsurance rates, the mixed plan and HMO arm, and what the individual deductible plan actually was (95% outpatient coinsurance, capped at $150 a person or $450 a family). It also says how the extract codes that plan, as 0% or 100%.
- **Assignment:** "random within site" → "randomly assigned".
- **Attrition caveat:** "this extract records only those who stayed" → "the extract does not identify who refused or dropped out".
- **Departures from the plan, in Methods:**
  - The plan was written after I had looked at summary statistics.
  - The confidence interval uses an exact transformation, not the planned delta method.
  - The 25% and 50% predictions were added after the results were seen. Table 2a's note says this too.
- **New caveats:**
  - The predictions combine coinsurance rates with out-of-pocket caps (`fmde`) that no plan actually had.
  - The `fmde` documentation conflicts with the data.
  - The coinsurance coefficient is estimated partly from the 1,074 deductible-plan person-years coded at 100%.

**Exploratory section:** Table 3 and the joint p-value now show the corrected numbers. I removed the claim that the less healthy "may respond somewhat less to the deductible". It now says neither difference comes close to significance.

**Still to do:**
- **Your notes:** `notes/references.md` has the same wrong Deb & Trivedi page range. I didn't change your notes.
- **Length:** the memo is now about 2,250 words, over the three pages you asked for. I'll trim it once the main-model question is settled.
- **Main model:** you asked for a model built for the zeros and extra spread, but it is still Poisson. Should I keep Poisson, or switch to negative binomial or, as I'd recommend, a hurdle negative binomial?
